Edge Rigidity of Dyson Brownian Motion with General Initial Data
Amol Aggarwal 1,2, Jiaoyang Huang 3

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the edge behavior of Dyson Brownian motion with general β \beta . Specifically, we consider the scenario where the averaged initial density near the edge, on the scale η ∗ \eta_{*} , is lower bounded by a square root profile. Under this assumption, we establish that the fluctuations of extreme particles are bounded by ( log n ) 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) n − 2 / 3 (\log n)^{\OO(1)}n^{-2/3} after time C ​ η ∗ C\sqrt{\eta_{*}} . Our result improves previous edge rigidity results from [ 24 , 1 ] which require both lower and upper bounds of the averaged initial density. Additionally, combining with [ 24 ] , our rigidity estimates are used to prove that the distribution of extreme particles converges to the Tracy-Widom β \beta distribution in short time.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Preface

In 1962
[
17
]
, Dyson interpreted the Gaussian orthogonal and unitary ensembles as dynamical limit of the matrix valued Brownian motion, which is given by

(1.1)

\displaystyle{\rm d}H(t)={\rm d}B(t),

where
B(t)
is the Brownian motion on real symmetric or complex Hermitian matrices. It turns out the eigenvalues of the above matrix valued Brownian motion satisfy a system of stochastic differential equations. These equations have been later generalized to stochastic differential equations, called the
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motion,

(1.2)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\lambda_{i}(t)=\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}{\rm d}B_{i}(t)+\frac{1}{n}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}t}{\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)},\quad 1\leq i\leq n,

where
{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}(t)={\bm{\lambda}}(t)=(\lambda^{(n)}_{1}(t),\lambda^{(n)}_{2}(t),\cdots,\lambda_{n}^{(n)}(t))=(\lambda_{1}(t),\lambda_{2}(t),\cdots,\lambda_{n}(t))\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
lies in the closure of the Weyl chamber
\mathbb{W}_{n}=\{\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:y_{1}>y_{2}>\cdots>y_{n}\}
and
\{B_{i}(t)\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
are independent Brownian motions.
The real symmetric and complex Hermitian matrix valued Brownian motion corresponds to (
1.2
) with
\beta=1
and
\beta=2
respectively.

Dyson Brownian motion is a prototype model for a one-dimension interacting particle system with singular interactions, which capture the statistical properties of energy levels in complex systems. The understanding of Dyson Brownian motion plays an important role in the proof of Wigner’s original universality conjecture by Erdős, Schlein and Yau
[
18
]
. For a review about the general framework regarding the proof of the universality conjecture, one can read the book
[
19
]
.

In this paper, we investigate the edge behavior of Dyson Brownian motion (DBM) described by equation (
1.2
), considering various initial data. If the initial data is a delta mass at zero, the law of Dyson Brownian motion at time one corresponds to the Gaussian-
\beta
-ensemble distribution. Notably, the largest particle fluctuates exhibits fluctuations on the scale of
n^{-2/3}
, commonly referred to as optimal edge rigidity. Moreover, its fluctuations converge to the Tracy-Widom
\beta
distribution
[
27
,
11
,
22
]
. A natural question arises:
when starting from general initial data, how long does it take to observe optimal edge rigidity, characterized by
n^{-2/3}
fluctuations?

It has been proven in
[
24
]
(
\beta=1,2
) and
[
1
]
(general
\beta
), if the initial data has a spectral edge with square root
behavior down to a scale
\eta_{*}
, then after time
C\sqrt{\eta_{*}}
, the extreme particle concentrates on the scale
n^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
.
However, when the initial data contains an outlier, the extreme eigenvalue initially experiences fluctuations on the scale of
n^{-1/2}
, and after time
\OO(1)
, it merges with the bulk of the spectral distribution, resulting in
n^{-2/3+\oo(1)}
fluctuations. This phenomenon is commonly referred to as the Baik–Ben Arous–Péché (BBP) phase transition
[
5
,
7
,
8
]
.

In this paper, we propose a general criterion that encompasses both aforementioned cases and is believed to be necessary (up to constants). Specifically, we demonstrate that if on the scale
\eta_{*}
, the averaged density near the edge is lower bounded by a square root profile (without imposing the analogous upper bound), then the fluctuations of extreme particles are bounded by
(\log n)^{\OO(1)}n^{-2/3}
after time
C\sqrt{\eta_{*}}
. Combining with
[
24
]
, our optimal edge rigidity estimates are used to give a proof of the local ergodicity of Dyson Brownian motion for more general initial data, i.e., the distribution of extreme particles converges to Tracy-Widom
\beta
distribution in short time.

The limiting empirical particle density of Dyson Brownian motion can be expressed as the free convolution of the initial density with the rescaled semicircle distribution, which corresponds to the solution of a complex Burgers equation
[
4
]
. To establish the edge rigidity, we employ a refined comparison between the empirical particle density of Dyson Brownian motion and a deterministic measure-valued process represented by the solution of the complex Burgers equation. This solution can be obtained using the method of characteristics. The Stieltjes transform of the empirical particle density satisfies the same complex Burgers equation, with the addition of a stochastic term and some small error terms. By plugging the characteristic flow of the complex Burgers equation, we can analyze the differences between the two Stieltjes transforms using estimates of Grönwall type. The method of characteristics has previously been employed in different contexts related to Dyson Brownian motion. In
[
20
]
, it was utilized to investigate the bulk behavior of Dyson Brownian motion. In
[
1
]
, it was applied to study the edge behavior of Dyson Brownian motion when starting from a profile exhibiting square root behavior. Furthermore, the method of characteristics was utilized in
[
9
]

to study the extreme gaps between eigenvalues of Wigner matrices, and in
[
2
]
, to analyze particle rigidity in unitary Dyson Brownian motion.

Our case poses additional challenges as we only assume a lower bound for the averaged initial density near the edge, without assuming square root behavior. As a crucial initial step, we establish that if the averaged initial density near the edge satisfies this lower bound, it continues to hold within a finite time frame. To prove this, we develop a coupling technique for Dyson Brownian motions (see
2.10
) with different initial data. This coupling ensures that if one Dyson Brownian motion exhibits larger gaps between particles at the beginning, this property persists for all subsequent times. Notably, this coupling technique may have independent significance beyond our specific study.

In previous work
[
1
]
, a key insight relied on the observation that the characteristic flows near the edge progress at a slower rate compared to the movement of the spectral edge, which is easily obtained when the density has square root behavior. In our case, we undertake a more meticulous analysis of the equations governing the relative movement of our characteristics with respect to the spectral edge. As a result, we establish two quantitative versions (see
3.2
and
3.3
) of these estimates when the averaged density near the edge is lower bounded by a square root profile. These two estimates, combined with a stopping time argument, enable us to prove that the Stieltjes transform of the empirical particle density remains small outside the right edge of the free-convolution density. Consequently, there are no outliers, and optimal edge rigidity is guaranteed.

Notation.

Let
\mathbb{H}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Imaginary z>0\}
denote the upper half-plane and
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
denote its closure. For any real numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
, we set
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
. We will write
X=\OO(Y)
to mean that
|X|\leq CY
for some universal constant
C>0
. We also write
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
and
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
.
Let
\mathscr{P}_{\fin}=\mathscr{P}_{\fin}(\mathbb{R})
denote the set of nonnegative measures
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
with finite total mass,
\mu(\mathbb{R})<\infty
. Further let
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
denote the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
; the support of any measure
\nu\in\mathscr{P}
is denoted by
\supp\nu
. We say that a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}
has density
\varrho
(with respect to Lebesgue measure) if
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function satisfying
\mu(dx)=\varrho(x)dx
. For any real number
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\delta_{x}\in\mathscr{P}
denote the delta function at
x
. Given two measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
of finite total mass, the Lévy distance between them is

(1.3)

\displaystyle d_{{\rm L}}(\mu,\nu)=\inf\Bigg\{a>0:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{y-a}\mu(dx)-a\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{y}\nu(dx)\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{y+a}\mu(dx)+a,\quad\text{for all $y\in\mathbb{R}$}\Bigg\}.

For two sets
A,B\in{\mathbb{R}}
we denote
\dist(A,B)=\inf_{x\in A,y\in B}|x-y|
. When
A=\{x\}
is a single point, we simply write
\dist(\{x\},B)=\dist(x,B)
.
We denote
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
and for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
make the convention

(1.4)

\displaystyle+\infty>\lambda>-\infty,\quad\infty-\lambda,\lambda-(-\infty)=+\infty,\quad(-\infty)-\lambda,\lambda-\infty=-\infty.

Organization.

We present the main results in the rest of
Section

1
.
In
Section

2
, we collect some preliminary results on free convolution with semicircle distributions, previous results on Dyson Brownian motion and couplings of Dyson Brownian motions with different initial data.
In
Section

3
, we prove our main results on the edge rigidity and universality of Dyson Brownian motion from general initial data. Finally in
Section

4
, we discuss some examples and applications of our optimal rigidity result.

1.2.
Main Results

We fix
\beta\geq 1
and consider Dyson Brownian motion (
1.2
) with general initial data.
We denote the empirical particle density of the
n
-particle Dyson Brownian motion
{\bm{\lambda}}(t)
by

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(t)},\quad t\geq 0.

Remark 1.1
.

In some cases (we will state when explicitly), we allow some particles to be at
\pm\infty
. More precisely, we allow for some
1\leq\ell\leq m\leq n
to have
\lambda_{1}(t)=\lambda_{2}(t)=\cdots\lambda_{\ell-1}(t)=+\infty
and
\lambda_{m+1}(t)=\lambda_{m+2}(t)=\cdots\lambda_{n}(t)=-\infty
. In this case, recalling our convention (
1.4
), (
1.2
) is a Dyson Brownian motion with
m-\ell+1
particles, indexed by
\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
. In this case, the empirical density is a measure valued process, with total mass
\mu_{t}^{(n)}({\mathbb{R}})=(m-\ell+1)/n
.

We study
n
-particle Dyson Brownian motion, with initial data

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(0)}.

Assumption 1.2
.

Fix constants
\mathsf{T}\geq 100
and
\mathsf{b}\in(0,1)
. Take a small parameter
\eta_{*}=\eta_{*}(n)\in(0,1/4)
(possibly depending on
n
). Assume the initial data
\mu_{0}^{(n)}
(as in (
1.6
)) is supported on
[-\infty,0]
, satisfying
\mu_{0}^{(n)}([-x,0])\geq{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}
for all
\eta_{*}\leq x\leq{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
.

We denote by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}^{(n)}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
the free convolution of
\mu_{0}^{(n)}
and the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
(see (
2.5
)); see
Section

2.1
for a more detailed definition.
Adopt
1.2
. We will prove in
2.14
, that for
(30/{\mathsf{b}})\sqrt{\eta_{*}}\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
,
\mu_{t}({\rm d}x)=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
has square root behavior close to the right edge
E_{t}:=\sup(\supp\mu_{t})
of the support of
\mu_{t}
, that is,

(1.7)

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x=(1+\OO(|E_{t}-x|))\frac{\sqrt{{\mathcal{A}}(t)(E_{t}-x)}}{\pi},\quad x\rightarrow E_{t},

where
{\mathcal{A}}(t)={\mathcal{A}}^{\mu_{0}}(t)
is a scalar depending on
\mu_{0}
and
t
. We refer to
2.14
for the explicit formula.

Theorem 1.3
.

Adopt
1.2
, and let
{\mathfrak{B}}=2^{60}{\mathsf{b}}^{-6}>0
. Then there exists a large
C=C({\mathsf{b}},{\mathsf{T}})>1
such that the following holds with probability
1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}
. For all
t\in[{\mathfrak{B}}\sqrt{\eta_{*}},{\mathsf{T}}]
, we have

(1.8)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(t)\leq E_{t}+\frac{(\log n)^{15}}{n^{2/3}}.

Theorem 1.4
.

Adopt
1.2
; fix real numbers
0<{\mathfrak{a}}<1\leq\Theta
and a smooth, compactly supported test function
F:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
; and let
{\mathfrak{B}}=2^{60}{\mathsf{b}}^{-6}>0
and
{\mathfrak{t}}=\max\{2{\mathfrak{B}}\sqrt{\eta_{*}},n^{-1/3+{\mathfrak{a}}}\}
. There exists a constant
\delta=\delta(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{T},\mathfrak{a},\Theta)>0
such that the following holds for any sufficiently large integer
n
. If for each
{\mathfrak{t}}/2\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
we have
{\mathcal{A}}(t)\in[\Theta^{-1},\Theta]
(recall (
1.7
)) and
\Theta^{-1}\sqrt{x}\leq\varrho_{t}(E_{t}-x)\leq\Theta\sqrt{x}
for
0\leq x\leq\Theta^{-1}
,
then

(1.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[F(n^{2/3}{\mathcal{A}}(t)^{1/3}(E_{t}-\lambda_{1}))]=\mathbb{E}_{\emph{Airy}_{\beta}}[F(\Lambda_{1})]+\OO\left(n^{-\delta}\right),\quad\text{for any}\quad{\mathfrak{t}}\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}-\Theta,\end{split}

where the second expectation is with respect to the Tracy–Widom
\beta
distribution
TW_{\beta}
of
[
27
, Theorem 1.3]
.

The proofs of
1.3
and
1.4
will be given in
Section

3
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Free Convolution With Semicircle Distributions

In this section we recall various results concerning Stieltjes transforms and free convolutions with the semicircle distribution. To that end, fix a finite measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
. We define the
Stieltjes transform
of
\mu
to be the function
m=m^{\mu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
for any complex number
z\in\mathbb{H}
setting

(2.1)

\displaystyle m(z)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu({\rm d}x)}{x-z}.

We have the following estimates on the Stieltjes transform and its derivatives.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
m(z)=m_{\mu}(z)
be the Stieltjes transform of a finite measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{\rm fin}
with
A=\mu({\mathbb{R}})
. For any integer
p\geq 1
, we denote its
p
-th derivative by
m^{(p)}(z)
. Then,

(2.2)

\displaystyle|m(z)|\leq\frac{A}{\dist(z,\supp(\mu))},\quad\quad|m^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{{\rm Im}[m(z)]}{{\rm Im}[z]},\quad|m^{(p)}(z)|\leq\frac{p!A}{\dist(z,\supp(\mu))^{p+1}}

Proof.

The Stieltjes transform and its derivatives are given by

\displaystyle|m(z)|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu(x)}{x-z}\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu(x)}{|z-x|}\leq\frac{A}{\dist(z,\supp(\mu))},

and

\displaystyle|m^{(p)}(z)|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{p!{\rm d}\mu(x)}{(x-z)^{p+1}}\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{p!{\rm d}\mu(x)}{|z-x|^{p+1}}\leq\frac{p!A}{\dist(z,\supp(\mu))^{p+1}}.

Finally for the middle statement of (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle|m^{\prime}(z)|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu(x)}{(x-z)^{2}}\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu(x)}{|z-x|^{2}}=\frac{1}{{\rm Im}[z]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm Im}[z]{\rm d}\mu(x)}{|z-x|^{2}}=\frac{{\rm Im}[m(z)]}{{\rm Im}[z]}.

∎

If
\mu
has a density with respect to Lebesgue measure, that is,
\mu({\rm d}x)=\varrho(x){\rm d}x
for some
\varrho\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\varrho
can be recovered from its Stieltjes transform by the identity
[
26
, Equation (8.14)]
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}\displaystyle\lim_{y\rightarrow 0}\Imaginary m(x+\mathrm{i}y)=\varrho(x);\qquad\displaystyle\pi^{-1}\lim_{y\rightarrow 0}\Real m(x+\mathrm{i}y)=H\varrho(x),

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. In the latter,
Hf
denotes the Hilbert transform of any function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, given by

(2.4)

\displaystyle Hf(x)=\pi^{-1}\cdot\PV\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{f(w)dw}{w-x},

where
\PV
denotes the Cauchy principal value (assuming the integral exists as a principal value).

The
semicircle distribution
is a measure
\mu_{\semci}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R})
whose density
\varrho_{\semci}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure is given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\semci}(x)=\displaystyle\frac{(4-x^{2})^{1/2}}{2\pi}\cdot\textbf{1}_{x\in[-2,2]},\qquad\text{for all $x\in\mathbb{R}$}.

For any real number
t>0
, we denote the rescaled semicircle density
\varrho_{\semci}^{(t)}
and distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}\in\mathscr{P}
by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\semci}^{(t)}(x)=t^{-1/2}\varrho_{\semci}(t^{-1/2}x);\qquad\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}=\varrho_{\semci}^{(t)}(x)dx.

We next discuss the free convolution of a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}
with the (rescaled) semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. For any
t>0
, denote the function
M=M^{\mu}=M^{t;\mu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and the set
\Lambda_{t}=\Lambda_{t;\mu}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
by (see
Figure

1
)

(2.7)

\displaystyle M(z)=z-tm(z);\quad\Lambda_{t}=\Big\{z\in\mathbb{H}:\Imaginary\big(z-tm(z)\big)>0\Big\}=\Bigg\{z\in\mathbb{H}:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu(dx)}{|z-x|^{2}}<\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\Bigg\}.

Figure 1.
\Lambda_{t}
as defined in (
2.7
) is an open subset of the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
.
M(z)
is a holomorphic map from
\Lambda_{t}
to
\mathbb{H}
.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
6
, Lemma 4]
)
.

The function
M
is a homeomorphism from
\overline{\Lambda}_{t}
to
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
. Moreover, it is a holomorphic map from
\Lambda_{t}
to
\mathbb{H}
and a bijection from
\partial\Lambda_{t}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

For any real number
t\geq 0
, define
m_{t}=m_{t}^{\mu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
as follows. First set
m_{0}(z)=m(z)
; for any real number
t>0
, define
m_{t}
so that

(2.8)

\displaystyle m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)=m_{0}(z),\qquad\text{for any $z\in\Lambda_{t}$}.

Since by
2.2
the function
M(z)=z-tm_{0}(z)
is a bijection from
\Lambda_{t}
to
\mathbb{H}
, (
2.8
) defines
m_{t}
on
\mathbb{H}
. By
[
6
, Proposition 2]
,
m_{t}
is the Stieltjes transform of a measure
\mu_{t}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R})
. This measure is called the
free convolution
between
\mu
and
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
, and we often write
\mu_{t}=\mu\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. By
[
6
, Corollary 2]
,
\mu_{t}
has a density
\varrho_{t}=\varrho_{t}^{\mu}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with respect to Lebesgue measure for
t>0
.

In the following lemma, we collect some properties of free convolution with rescaled semicircle distributions; it mainly follows from
[
6
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The following statements hold, for any real number
t>0
.

(1)

Define the function
v_{t}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by for each
u\in\mathbb{R}
setting

(2.9)

\displaystyle v_{t}(u)=\displaystyle\inf\bigg\{v\geq 0:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu(dx)}{(u-x)^{2}+v^{2}}\leq t^{-1}\bigg\}.

Then
v_{t}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the boundary of
\Lambda_{t}
is parameterized by
\partial\Lambda_{t}=\{E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E):E\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
, and the set
\big\{E\in{\mathbb{R}}:v_{t}(E)>0\big\}
consists of countably many open intervals
\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
.

(a)

For each
E\in\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
, we have
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big|x-E-\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big|^{-2}\mu(dx)=t^{-1}
.

(b)

For each
E\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
, we have
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big|x-E-\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big|^{-2}\mu(dx)\leq t^{-1}
.

(2)

We have
\supp\mu\subseteq\bigcup_{i\geq 1}[a_{i},b_{i}]
, and
\mu\big(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\{a_{i},b_{i}\}\big)=0
.

(3)

The function
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is (strictly) increasing in
E\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover,
v_{t}(E)
and
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
are smooth for
E
in the interior of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\{a_{i},b_{i}\}
.

(4)

A real number
y\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\varrho_{t}(y)>0
if and only if
y=M(w)=w-tm_{0}(w)
for some
w=w(y)\in\partial\Lambda_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}
. Moreover, the function
w(y)
is smooth in
y\in\big\{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\varrho_{t}(y^{\prime})>0\big\}
.

(a)

We have
\varrho_{t}(y)=\pi^{-1}\Imaginary m_{0}(w)=(\pi t)^{-1}\Imaginary w
.

(b)

The Hilbert transform of
\varrho_{t}
is given by
H\varrho_{t}(y)=\pi^{-1}\Real m_{0}(w)=(\pi t)^{-1}\Real(w-y)
.

(5)

Denote
\mathfrak{e}_{+}=\mathfrak{e}_{+}(t)=\max(\supp\mu_{t})
and
\mathfrak{e}_{-}=\mathfrak{e}_{-}(t)=\min\supp\mu_{t}
, and let

\displaystyle w_{+}=\sup\Bigg\{w\in\mathbb{R}:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu(dx)}{(x-w)^{2}}>t^{-1}\Bigg\};\qquad w_{-}=\inf\Bigg\{w\in\mathbb{R}:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu(dx)}{(x-w)^{2}}>t^{-1}\Bigg\}.

Then
\mathfrak{e}_{+}=w_{+}-tm_{0}(w_{+})
and
\mathfrak{e}_{-}=w_{-}-tm_{0}(w_{-})
.

(6)

For any real number
t>0
, both
\varrho_{t}(y)
and its Hilbert transform
H\varrho_{t}(y)
are smooth in
y\in\big\{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\varrho_{t}(y^{\prime})>0\big\}
. Moreover, we have

(2.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}(y)=\pi\cdot\partial_{y}\big(\varrho_{t}(y)H\varrho_{t}(y)\big),\qquad\text{for $y\in\big\{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\varrho_{t}(y^{\prime})>0\big\}$}.

Proof.

Item

1
of the lemma follows from
[
6
, Lemma 2]
and the content in
[
6
]
directly following it (the fact that
\big\{E\in\mathbb{R}:v_{t}(E)>0\big\}
is open, implying that it consists of countably many open intervals, follows from the definition (
2.9
) of
v_{t}
). To establish
Item

2
, assume to the contrary that
\mu\big(\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})\big)>0
. Then, there exists some constant
c>0
and subset
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
such that
\mu(I)\geq c
and
I\subseteq[-c^{-1},c^{-1}]
. Denoting for any integers
n\geq 1
and
j\in\llbracket-n,n-1\rrbracket
the subsets
I_{j;n}=I\cap\big[j/cn,(j+1)/cn\big]
, it follows for each
n\geq 1
that there exists some
j_{n}\in\llbracket-n,n-1\rrbracket
such that
\mu(I_{j_{n};n})\geq c/2n
. Hence, for any real number
E_{n}\in I_{j_{n};n}\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle t^{-1}\geq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x-E_{n}|^{-2}\mu(dx)\geq\displaystyle\int_{I_{j_{n};n}}|x-E_{n}|^{-2}\mu(dx)\geq c^{2}n^{2}\mu(I_{j_{n};n})\geq\displaystyle\frac{c^{3}n}{2},

where in the first inequality we used
Item

1
of the lemma; in the second we restricted the integral to
I_{j_{n};n}
; in the third we used the bound
|x-E|\leq(cn)^{-1}
for each
x,E\in I_{j_{n};n}
; and in the fourth we used the fact that
\mu(I_{j_{n};n})>c/2n
. Taking
n>2(tc^{3})^{-1}
gives a contradiction, implying
Item

2
of the lemma.

To verify
Item

3
of the lemma, recall from
2.2
that
M
is a continuous bijection from
\partial\Lambda_{t}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Together with
Item

1
of the lemma, this implies that
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is either (strictly) increasing or (strictly) decreasing. By
[
6
, Lemma 5]
,
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is (strictly) increasing on
\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
(which is nonempty by
Item

2
of the lemma), meaning that
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is (strictly) increasing on
\mathbb{R}
; this verifies the first statement in
Item

3
of the lemma. The fact that
v_{t}
is smooth on
\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
follows from
[
6
, Lemma 2]
; since
v_{t}=0
in the interior of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
, it follows that
v_{t}
is smooth in the interior of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\{a_{i},b_{i}\}
. This also implies that
M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is smooth in the interior of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
(since, by (
2.1
),
m\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
is smooth in
v_{t}(E)
, as long as
v_{t}(E)>0
or
E\notin\supp\mu
), verifying
Item

3
of the lemma.

All statements in
Item

4
of the lemma, except for the smoothness of
w
in
y
, follow from
[
6
, Corollary 2, Corollary 3]
. To show this smoothness, we use
Item

1
of the lemma to express
w=E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)
for some real number
E\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
y(E)=M(w)=M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)
, which is smooth in
E
by
Item

3
of the lemma, which also indicates that
\partial_{E}M\big(E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)\big)>0
on the domain where
v_{t}(E)>0
. So, the implicit function theorem implies that
w=E+\mathrm{i}v_{t}(E)
is smooth in
y
when
v_{t}(E)>0
(that is, when
w\in\mathbb{H}
, or equivalently when
\varrho_{t}(y)>0
). Then,
Item

5
of the lemma follows from
Item

4
and the definition (
2.9
) of
v_{t}
.

The first part of
Item

6
follows from
[
6
, Corollary 3]
(together with
[
6
, Lemma 2]
). For the second part of
Item

6
, by taking the derivative on both sides of (
2.8
) with respect to
t
, we get for any
z\in\Lambda_{t}
that

\displaystyle 0=\partial_{t}m_{0}(z)=\partial_{t}\Big(m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)-\partial_{z}m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)\cdot m_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)-\partial_{z}m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)\cdot m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big).

Setting
w=z-tm_{0}(z)
and applying
2.2
, it follows for any
w=y+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{H}
that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}m_{t}(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\partial_{z}\big(m_{t}(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)\big)^{2}.

Sending
\eta
to zero and applying (
2.3
) (with the first part of the lemma), we find

(2.11)

\displaystyle\pi\cdot\partial_{t}\big(H\varrho_{t}(y)+\mathrm{i}\varrho_{t}(y)\big)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\partial_{y}\Big(\big(H\varrho_{t}(y)+\mathrm{i}\varrho_{t}(y)\big)^{2}\Big).

Then (
2.10
) follows from comparing the imaginary parts on both sides of (
2.11
).
∎

Remark 2.4
.

While free convolutions are typically defined between probability measures, the relation (
2.8
) also defines the free convolution of any measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
, satisfying
A=\mu(\mathbb{R})<\infty
, with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. Indeed, define the probability measure
\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathscr{P}
from
\mu
by setting
\widetilde{\mu}(I)=A^{-1}\cdot\mu(A^{1/2}I)
, for any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, for any real number
s\geq 0
, define the probability measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{s}=\widetilde{\mu}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(s)}
, and denote its Stieltjes transform by
\widetilde{m}_{s}
. Then, define the free convolution
\mu_{t}=\mu\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
and its Stieltjes transform
m_{t}=m_{t;\mu}
by setting

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(I)=A\cdot\widetilde{\mu}_{t}(A^{-1/2}I),\qquad\text{for any interval $I\subseteq\mathbb{R}$, so that}\qquad m_{t}(z)=A^{1/2}\cdot\widetilde{m}_{t}(A^{-1/2}z),

where the second equality follows from the first by (
2.1
). Then,

\displaystyle m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)=A^{1/2}\cdot\widetilde{m}_{t}\Big(A^{-1/2}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=A^{1/2}\cdot\widetilde{m}_{t}\big(A^{-1/2}z-t\widetilde{m}_{0}(A^{-1/2}z)\big)

\displaystyle=A^{1/2}\cdot\widetilde{m}_{0}(A^{-1/2}z)=m_{0}(z),

so that (
2.8
) continues to hold for
m_{t}
. In particular,
2.2
and
2.3
also hold for
\mu
.

Example 2.5
.

If we start from the delta mass
\mu_{0}=A\delta_{0}
, then the free convolution
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
is supported on
[-2\sqrt{At},2\sqrt{At}]
, and it is given explicitly by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}({\rm d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{4At-x^{2}}}{2\pi t}{\rm d}x,\quad t\geq 0.

2.2.
Empirical measure of Dyson Brownian motion

It is known that for
n
-particle Dyson Brownian motion, if the initial data (
1.6
) weakly converges to a probability measure
\mu
, then the empirical particle density (
1.5
) (as a measure valued process) converges to the free convolution of
\mu
with the rescaled semicircle distribution. We recall the precise statement from
[
4
]
.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
4
, Proposition 4.3.10, Corollary 4.3.11]
)
.

Fix a compactly supported probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}
and real number
{\mathsf{T}}>1
. Denote the free convolution of
\mu
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}\in\mathscr{P}
by
\mu_{t}
.
For each integer
n\geq 1
, let
{\bm{u}}^{(n)}=(u^{(n)}_{1},u^{(n)}_{2},\cdots,u^{(n)}_{n})\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
denote sequences such that the empirical measures
(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{(n)}\delta_{u^{(n)}_{i}}
converge weakly to
\mu
.
Consider
n
particle Dyson Brownian motion
\bm{\lambda}^{(n)}(t)=(\lambda_{1}^{(n)}(t),\lambda_{2}^{(n)}(t),\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{(n)}(t))
starting from
{\bm{u}}^{(n)}
. We denote the empirical particle density as
\mu_{t}^{n}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{(n)}\delta_{\lambda_{i}^{(n)}(t)}
. Then, for any real number
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}}d_{{\rm L}}(\mu_{t}^{(n)},\mu_{t})>\varepsilon\big]=0
, where
d_{\text{L}}
is the Lévy metric from (
1.3
).

Remark 2.7
.

In
2.6
, we can allow some particles to be at
\pm\infty
, as in
Remark

1.1
. Assume the initial data

(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{u^{n}_{i}}
converges weakly to a positive measure
\mu
with
\mu({\mathbb{R}})\leq 1
. Let
\mu_{t}
be the free convolution of
\mu
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}\in\mathscr{P}
as in
2.4
. Then
2.6
still holds.

A stronger version of convergence has been proven in
[
20
]
, which proves that the optimal bulk rigidity holds for Dyson Brownian motion with general potentials. To state it, we need some further notation.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
denote a finite measure with
\mu({\mathbb{R}})=A
. We denote the associated inverted cumulative density
\gamma:[0,A]\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
, as

(2.13)

\displaystyle\gamma(y)=\displaystyle\sup\Bigg\{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}:\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}^{\infty}{\rm d}\mu(x)\geq y\Bigg\}.

We also set
\gamma(y)=\infty
if
y<0
, and
\gamma(y)=-\infty
if
y>A
. Then
\gamma(y)
is non-increasing and left continuous, i.e.,
\gamma(y^{-})=\gamma(y)
.
For any integers
n\geq 1
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the
classical location
(also called
n^{-1}
-quantiles
) with respect to
\mu
,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}^{\mu}=\gamma_{j;n}=\gamma_{j;n}^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}=\gamma((j-1/2)/n),

for
j\in\llbracket 1,An\rrbracket
. We also set
\gamma_{j}=\infty
if
j<1
and
\gamma_{j}=-\infty
if
j>An
.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
20
, Corollary 3.2]
)
.

For any real number
D>1
, there exists a constant
C=C(D)>1
such that the following holds.
Consider
n
particle Dyson Brownian motion
\bm{\lambda}(t)=(\lambda_{1}(t),\lambda_{2}(t),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(t))
starting from
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}^{(n)}
with
\supp\mu_{0}\subset[-n^{D},n^{D}]
. We denote by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
the free convolution of
\mu_{0}
with rescaled semicircle distribution, and its classical locations by
\{\gamma_{i}(t)\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
(as in (
2.14
)). Then, with probability
1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}
, we have for all
0\leq t\leq n^{D}
and
1\leq i\leq n
that

(2.15)

\displaystyle\gamma_{i+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}(t)-n^{-D}\leq\lambda_{i}(t)\leq\gamma_{i-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}(t)+n^{-D}.

We mention that, In
[
20
]
, the probability on the right side of (
2.15
) was written to be
1-Cn^{-D}
for any
D>1
, but it can be seen from the proof (see that of
[
21
, Proposition 3.8]
, where
\delta
there is
\frac{5}{4}
here) that it can be taken to be
1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}
instead.

2.3.
Dyson Brownian Motion Comparison

In this section, we allow some particles to be at
\pm\infty
as in
Remark

1.1
. The following lemma gives
i
-th particle location and gap comparisons between Dyson Brownian motions with different initial data. The proof utilizes a coupling of Dyson Brownian motions and a maximum principle, which has been used previously in the random matrix literature
[
14
,
9
]
.

Figure 2.
Left Panel: if the initial data satisfies
\lambda_{i}(0)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)
, then we can couple the two Dyson Brownian motions
\lambda_{i}(t)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)
(with possibly some particles at
\pm\infty
).
Right Panel: if the initial data satisfies
\lambda_{i}(0)-\lambda_{j}(0)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(0)
, then we can couple the two Dyson Brownian motions
\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Take any integers
1\leq\ell\leq m\leq n
.
Consider two coupled Dyson Brownian motions (see
Figure

2
)

(2.16)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\lambda_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}t}{\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)}+\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}{\rm d}B_{i}(t),

(2.17)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}t}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)}+\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}{\rm d}B_{i}(t),

which share the same Brownian motions
\{B_{i}(t)\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
.

(1)

Assume that
\lambda_{i}(t)=+\infty
for
i\in\llbracket 1,\ell-1\rrbracket
and
\lambda_{i}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
i\in\llbracket\ell,n\rrbracket
, and that
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)=-\infty
for
i\in\llbracket m+1,n\rrbracket
. If
\lambda_{i}(0)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)
for all
\ell\leq i\leq m
, then
\lambda_{i}(t)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)
for all
\ell\leq i\leq m
and
t\geq 0
.

(2)

Assume that
\lambda_{i}(t)=+\infty
for
i\in\llbracket 1,\ell-1\rrbracket
;
\lambda_{i}(t)=-\infty
for
i\in\llbracket m+1,n\rrbracket
;
\lambda_{i}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
i\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
; and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. If
\lambda_{i}(0)-\lambda_{j}(0)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(0)
for all
\ell\leq i<j\leq m
, then
\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)
for all
\ell\leq i<j\leq m
and
t\geq 0
.

Proof of
2.10
.

By taking difference of (
2.16
) and (
2.17
), we have

(2.18)

\displaystyle{\rm d}(\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t))=-\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t))-(\lambda_{j}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)){\rm d}t}{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))},\quad\ell\leq i\leq m.

Let

i_{*}(t):=\argmin_{\ell\leq i\leq m}\{\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)\}

(if
i_{*}(t)
is not unique, we can simply take the smallest one).
By our assumption for
i\leq\ell-1
or
i\geq m+1
,
\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)=\infty
. Thus we also have

i_{*}(t)=\argmin_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)\}.

Since
\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)
are continuous,
i_{*}(t)
is piecewise constant. Either
i_{*}(t)
is continuous at
t
or
i_{*}(t)
has a jump at
t
. If
i_{*}(t)
is continuous at
t
, then
{\rm d}i_{*}(t)=0
and

(2.19)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t))}{{\rm d}t}=-\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq{i_{*}(t)}\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t))-(\lambda_{j}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))}{(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))}\geq 0,

where in the last inequality we used the definition of
i_{*}(t)
that
(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t))-(\lambda_{j}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))\leq 0
, and
(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))>0
.
If
i_{*}(t)
has a jump at
t
, then we have
\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t)=\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t)
, and as in (
2.19
) we have

(2.20)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}(\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t^{-})-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t^{-}))}{{\rm d}t},\frac{{\rm d}(\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t^{+})-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t^{+}))}{{\rm d}t}\geq 0.

We conclude from (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) that
\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)
is non-decreasing in
t
. Moreover by our assumption
\lambda_{i_{*}(0)}(0)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(0)}(0)\geq 0
. We conclude that for all
\ell\leq i\leq m
,

\lambda_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)\geq\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)\geq 0
for any
t\geq 0
. This finishes the first claim.

To prove the second claim, we further take the difference of (
2.18
) with
i
replaced by
i+1
,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\rm d}((\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t)))}{{\rm d}t}=-2\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))}{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))}\\
&-\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i,i+1\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t)}{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))(\lambda_{i+1}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))}-\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t)}{(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t))}.\end{split}

Let

i_{*}(t)=\argmin_{\ell\leq i\leq m-1}\{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))\}
(if
i_{*}(t)
is not unique, we can simply take the smallest one).
By our assumption for
i\leq\ell-1
or
i\geq m
,
(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))=\infty
. Thus we also have

i_{*}(t)=\argmin_{1\leq i\leq n}\{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))\}.

Since
(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t))
are continuous,
i_{*}(t)
is piecewise constant.
Either
i_{*}(t)
is continuous at
t
or
i_{*}(t)
has a jump at
t
.
If
i_{*}(t)
has a jump at
t
, then we have
(\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t)-\lambda_{{i_{*}(t^{-})}+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{-})}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(t^{-})}+1}(t))=(\lambda_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t)-\lambda_{{i_{*}(t^{+})}+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t^{+})}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(t^{+})}+1}(t))
. In both cases
(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\lambda_{{i_{*}(t)}+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(t)}+1}(t))
is continuous in
t
.

By our assumption
(\lambda_{i_{*}(0)}(0)-\lambda_{i_{*}(0)+1}(0))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(0)}(0)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(0)+1}(0))\geq 0
.
We denote the stopping time

(2.22)

\displaystyle\tau=\inf_{t\geq 0}\{t:(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\lambda_{i_{*}(t)+1}(t))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(t)+1}(t))<0\}.

Then either
\tau=\infty
, and our claim follows or
\tau<\infty
. Next we prove by contradiction that
\tau<\infty
is impossible. Otherwise

(2.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)+1}(\tau))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)+1}(\tau))=0,\\
&\lambda_{i}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau),\quad\ell\leq i\leq j\leq m,\end{split}

and there exists arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
such that

(2.24)

\displaystyle(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau+\varepsilon)}(\tau+\varepsilon)-\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau+\varepsilon)+1}(\tau+\varepsilon))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau+\varepsilon)}(\tau+\varepsilon)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau+\varepsilon)+1}(\tau+\varepsilon))<0.

By plugging
i=i_{*}(\tau)
(if
i_{*}(t)
has a jump at
\tau
, we interpret this as
i=i_{*}(\tau^{+})
) and
t=\tau
in (
2.21
), and using the first statement in (
2.23
), we get

(2.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{{\rm d}((\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)+1}(\tau))-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)+1}(\tau)))}{{\rm d}t}=-(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau))\times\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq{i_{*}(\tau)},{i_{*}(\tau)}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))(\lambda_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))}-\frac{1}{(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))}\geq 0,\end{split}

where we also used that
(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau))>0
and
0<(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))\leq(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))(\lambda_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))
from (
2.23
). If (
2.25
) is postive, then this contradicts (
2.24
).

Otherwise (
2.25
) equals zero, which is only possible if
(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))(\widetilde{\lambda}_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau))-(\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))(\lambda_{{i_{*}(\tau)}+1}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau))=0
for all
j\neq i_{*}(\tau),i_{*}(\tau)+1
. Together with (
2.23
), this implies that
\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{j}(\tau)=\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau)
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. In particular,
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(\tau)\}_{1\leq j\leq n}=\{\lambda_{j}(\tau)+c\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
for
c:=\widetilde{\lambda}_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)-\lambda_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the coupling (
2.16
) (notice that DBM only depends on the gaps
\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)
) implies that for any
t\geq\tau
, we have
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)\}_{1\leq j\leq n}=\{\lambda_{j}(t)+c\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
, which again contradicts the definition of
\tau
in (
2.22
).
∎

2.6
and
2.10
together give a continuous version of comparison for the free convolution with a rescaled semicircle distribution.

Lemma 2.11
.

Fix a real number
A\in(0,1]
, and finite measures
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathscr{P}_{\rm fin}
such that
\mu(\mathbb{R})=A
and
\widetilde{\mu}(\mathbb{R})=1
. Let
\mu_{t}=\mu\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}=\mu\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
denote the free convolutions of
\mu
and
\widetilde{\mu}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution respectively. Also denote the associated inverted cumulative density functions (recall (
2.13
)) by
\gamma_{t}:[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
respectively.

(1)

Assume for each
y\in[0,A]
that
\gamma_{0}(y)\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(y)
. Then,
\gamma_{t}(y)\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(y)
holds for each
(t,y)\in[0,\infty]\times[0,A]
.

(2)

Assume for each
y,y^{\prime}\in[0,A]
that
|\gamma_{0}(y)-\gamma_{0}(y^{\prime})|\geq|\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(y)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(y^{\prime})|
. Then,
|\gamma_{t}(y)-\gamma_{t}(y^{\prime})|\geq|\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(y)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(y^{\prime})|
holds for each
t\in[0,\infty]
and
y,y^{\prime}\in[0,A]
.

Proof of
2.11
.

Using
2.6
and
2.10
as input, the proofs of the two statements are similar. So we will only prove the second statement.
We take two sets of initial data, given by
\{\lambda_{i}(0)=\gamma_{0}((i-1/2)/n)\}_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor An\rfloor}
,
\{\lambda_{i}(0)=-\infty\}_{\lfloor An\rfloor<i\leq n}
and
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}((i-1/2)/n)\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Then for
any index
1\leq i\leq n
, by our assumption for
1\leq i\leq\lfloor An\rfloor
,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda_{i}(0)-\lambda_{i+1}(0)&=\gamma_{0}((i-1/2)/n)-\gamma_{0}((i+1/2)/n)\\
&\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}((i-1/2)/n)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}((i+1/2)/n)=\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(0)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(0).\end{split}

Then (
2.26
) verifies the assumptions in the second statement of
2.10
, so we can couple the two associated Dyson Brownion motions such that

(2.27)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{i+1}(t)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+1}(t),\quad 1\leq i\leq\lfloor An\rfloor.

By
2.6
(recalling the definitions of Lévy distance from (
1.3
) and the inverted cumulative density from (
2.13
)), we have for any
\varepsilon>0
,
0\leq a\leq A
, and sufficiently large
n\geq 1
that

(2.28)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(a+\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\leq\lambda_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t)\leq\gamma_{t}(a-\varepsilon)+\varepsilon,\quad\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a+\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\leq\widetilde{\lambda}_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t)\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a-\varepsilon)+\varepsilon,

In particular if
\gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
are both continuous at
a
, then we have

(2.29)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(a)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lambda_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t),\quad\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\lambda}_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t),

and for any
0\leq a<b\leq A
such that
\gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
are both continuous at
a,b
we have

(2.30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(a)-\gamma_{t}(b)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lambda_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t)-\lambda_{\lfloor bn\rfloor}(t)\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\lambda}_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(t)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{\lfloor bn\rfloor}(t)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(b).

Recall
\gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
are non-increasing and left continuous, by from the discussion after (
2.13
). They are thus continuous at all but countably many points. So for any
0<a<b\leq A
, we can take sequences
\{a_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},\{b_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
which converge to
a,b
respectively from below, such that
\gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
are both continuous at each
a_{k},b_{k}
.
Then it follows from (
2.30
) and the fact the
\gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
are left continuous

(2.31)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(a)-\gamma_{t}(b)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{t}(a_{k})-\gamma_{t}(b_{k})\geq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a_{k})-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(b_{k})=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(b).

If
a=0
, then
\gamma_{t}(a)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a)=\infty
and we have the trivial statement
\gamma_{t}(a)-\gamma_{t}(b)=\infty=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(a)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}(b)
.
This together with (
2.31
) finishes the proof of the second statement in (
2.11
).
∎

2.4.
Comparisons with Free Convolutions

In this section we discuss comparisons between Dyson Brownian motions with the classical locations of the free convolution, which will be used in
Section

4.2
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Fix
{\mathsf{T}}>0
.
Given a probability measure
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
, we denote by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
the free convolution of
\mu
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. We denote by
\gamma_{i}(t)=\gamma_{i;n}^{\mu_{t}}(t)
the classical locations of
\mu_{t}({\rm d}x)=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
(recall from (
2.14
)). We assume there exists a small constant
c>0
such that the following two statements hold for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
.

(1)

\mu_{t}=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
has a bounded density:
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\varrho_{t}(x)\leq(2c)^{-1}
.

(2)

The derivative of
\varrho_{t}(x)
is bounded in the following way: when
\dist(x,\{\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t),\cdots,\gamma_{n}(t)\})\leq c/2n
, we have
|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|\leq n/c
.

Then there exists
C=C(c,{\mathsf{T}})>1
such that the following holds for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}|\lambda_{i}(s)-\gamma_{i}(s)|\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq n}|\lambda_{j}(0)-\gamma_{j}(0)|+C\left(t+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}.

Proof of
2.12
.

We denote
{\mathfrak{e}}_{+}(t)=\max(\supp\mu_{t})
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{-}(t)=\min\supp\mu_{t}
, as in
Item

5
of
2.3
.
By our assumption
\mu_{t}=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
has a
bounded density, so the inverted cumulative density (recall from (
2.13
)) satisfies

(2.33)

\displaystyle y=\int_{\gamma_{t}(y)}^{\infty}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x.

By taking the time derivative of (
2.33
), and using
Item

6
in
2.3
, we get

(2.34)

\displaystyle\del_{t}\gamma_{t}(y)\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{t}(y))=\int_{\gamma_{t}(y)}^{\infty}\del_{t}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x=\int_{\gamma_{t}(y)}^{\infty}\pi\del_{x}(\varrho_{t}(x)H\varrho_{t}(x)){\rm d}x=-\pi\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{t}(y))H\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{t}(y)).

It follows that
\del_{t}\gamma_{t}(y)=-\pi H\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{t}(y))
.
In particular,
the classical locations of
\varrho_{t}
(recall from (
2.14
)) satisfy
\del_{t}\gamma_{i}(t)=-\pi H\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{i}(t))
. Next we show that the Hilbert transform on the right side of this equation can be approximated by

(2.35)

\displaystyle\del_{t}\gamma_{i}(t)=-\pi H\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{i}(t))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j:j\neq i}\frac{1}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t)}+\OO\left(1\right),\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

where
\OO(1)
depends on
c
.

Since the density
\varrho_{t}
is bounded by
1/(2c)
, we have
|\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i\pm 1}(t)|\geq c/n
, and
|\gamma_{i}(t)-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\pm}(t)|\geq c/n
(which follows from (
2.14
), as it implies that
\int_{{\mathfrak{e}}_{-}(t)}^{\gamma_{i}(t)}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x,\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)}^{{\mathfrak{e}}_{+}(t)}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x\geq 1/(2n)
). We consider the integral

(2.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\PV&\int_{\gamma_{i-1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i+1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+c/2n}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-c/2n}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+c/2n}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\OO\left(1\right)\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+c/2n}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)-\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{i}(t))}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\OO\left(1\right)=\OO\left(\frac{\max_{x:|x-\gamma_{i}(t)|\leq c/2n}|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|}{n}+1\right)=\OO\left(1\right),\end{split}

where the implicit error
\OO(1)
depends only on
c
. We used
in the third line that on
[\gamma_{i}(t)+c/2n,\gamma_{i-1}(t)]
and
[\gamma_{i+1}(t),\gamma_{i}(t)-c/2n]
we have
|1/(\gamma_{i}(t)-x)|\leq 1/(c/2n))=2n/c
, so

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\left|\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\right|+\left|\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-c/2n}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n}{c}\left|\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\varrho_{t}(x)dx\right|+\frac{2n}{c}\left|\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-c/2n}\varrho_{t}(x)dx\right|\leq\frac{2n}{c}\cdot\frac{1}{n}+\frac{2n}{c}\cdot\frac{1}{n}=\frac{4}{c}.

where we also used the definition of classical locations (recall from (
2.14
));
in the fourth line we used that
\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-c/2n}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+c/2n}(\gamma_{i}(t)-x)^{-1}dx=0
; in the last line, we used that for any
\dist(x,\{\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t),\cdots,\gamma_{n}(t)\})\leq c/2n
, it holds
|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|\leq n/c
from our assumption.
For the integral outside the interval
[\gamma_{i-1}(t),\gamma_{i+1}(t)]
, we have the trivial bounds

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}\leq\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\int_{\gamma_{j+1}(t)}^{\gamma_{j}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t)}\leq\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))},

and

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}\geq\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\int_{\gamma_{j+1}(t)}^{\gamma_{j}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j+1}(t)}\geq\sum_{j=i+2}^{n}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}.

Thus we conclude that

(2.37)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}-\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}\right|\leq\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i+1}(t))}\leq\frac{1}{c}.

We have the same estimate for the integral from
\gamma_{i-1}(t)
to
\infty
. The claim (
2.35
) follows from combining (
2.36
) and (
2.37
).

Next we show (
2.32
) using (
2.35
) as input. By taking the difference of (
1.2
) and (
2.35
), we get

(2.38)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm d}(\lambda_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t))&=\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}{\rm d}B_{i}(t)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j:j\neq i}\frac{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t))-(\lambda_{j}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}{(\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t))(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}{\rm d}t+\OO\left(1\right),\quad 1\leq i\leq n.\end{split}

We take
i_{*}(t)=\arg\max_{1\leq i\leq n}(\lambda_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t))
(if
i_{*}(t)
is not unique, we can simply take the smallest one). Since the
\lambda_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)
are continuous,
i_{*}(t)
is piecewise constant. By plugging
i=i_{*}(t)
(if
i_{*}
has a jump at
t
, we interpret this as either
i_{*}(t^{-})
or
i_{*}(t^{+})
) in (
2.38
), noticing that the second term on the righthand side of (
2.38
) is nonpositive, we deduce (through a very similar argument to the one used to obtain (
2.19
) and (
2.20
)) that

(2.39)

\displaystyle{\rm d}(\lambda_{i_{*}(t)}(t)-\gamma_{i_{*}(t)}(t))\leq\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}{\rm d}B_{i_{*}(t)}(t)+\OO\left(1\right){\rm d}t.

The martingale term
{\rm d}B_{i_{*}(t)}(t)
has the same law as a standard Brownian motion. Thus by the reflection principle we have

(2.40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}B_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)\geq a\sqrt{t}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}B_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)\geq a\sqrt{t}\right)\leq 2e^{-a^{2}/2}.

By integrating both sides of (
2.39
) (and taking
a=\log n
in the above tail estimate), we deduce that there exists a large constant
C=C(c,{\mathsf{T}})>0
such that, the following holds with probability
1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}
for any
0\leq s\leq t
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{*}(s)}(s)-\gamma_{i_{*}(s)}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{i_{*}(0)}(0)-\gamma_{i_{*}(0)}(0)+\Big(\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta n}\Big)^{1/2}\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}B_{i_{*}(\tau)}(\tau)+\OO(s)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(0)-\gamma_{i}(0)|+C\left(t+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

It follows that for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
and
n
large enough,

(2.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(s)-\gamma_{i}(s)\leq\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(0)-\gamma_{i}(0)|+C\left(t+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}.

By the same argument, we have a similar lower bound by considering
i_{*}(t)=\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq n}(\lambda_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t))
, that is, we have for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
and
n
large enough,

(2.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}\gamma_{i}(s)-\lambda_{i}(s)\leq\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(0)-\gamma_{i}(0)|+C\left(t+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}},

2.12
follows from combining (
2.41
) and (
2.42
).
∎

We also need the following special case of
2.12
, which compares Dyson Brownian motion starting from a delta mass with the quantiles of semicircle distribution.

Proposition 2.13
.

Fix a real number
{\mathsf{T}}>0
and integers
1\leq m\leq n
; let
A=m/n\in(0,1]
. Define the measure
\mu_{0}=A\delta_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
, and denote by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
the free convolution of
\mu
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. We denote by
\gamma_{i}(t)=\gamma_{i;n}^{\mu_{t}}(t)
the classical locations (recall from (
2.14
)) of
\mu_{t}({\rm d}x)=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
for
1\leq i\leq m
. We consider the
m
-particle Dyson Brownian motion (
1.2
) starting from
\lambda_{1}(0)=\lambda_{2}(0)=\cdots=\lambda_{m}(0)=0
(and
\lambda_{m+1}(0)=\cdots=\lambda_{n}(0)=-\infty
). Then there exists a constant
C=C({\mathsf{T}})>0
such that the following holds for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}

(2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}|\lambda_{i}(s)-\gamma_{i}(s)|\leq C\left(\sqrt{At}+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}},\\
&\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}|\lambda_{i}(s)|\leq C\left(\sqrt{At}+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}.\end{split}

Proof of
2.13
.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as in
2.12
, so we will only sketch the proof.
We recall from
Example

2.5
that the free convolution
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
is supported on
[-2\sqrt{At},2\sqrt{At}]
, and given explicitly by the rescaled semicircle distribution

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}({\rm d}x)=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x=\frac{\sqrt{4At-x^{2}}}{2\pi t}{\rm d}x,\quad t\geq 0.

One can directly check that
\varrho_{t}(x)
satisfies for
0\leq x\leq 2\sqrt{At}
the bounds

(2.45)

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}(x)\leq\varrho_{t}(0)=\frac{\sqrt{A/t}}{\pi}\leq\frac{\sqrt{A/t}}{2},\quad|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|\leq(2\sqrt{At}-x)^{-1/2}\frac{A^{1/4}}{t^{3/4}}.

Using the above estimates as input, similarly to (
2.35
), next we show

(2.46)

\displaystyle\del_{t}\gamma_{i}(t)=-\pi H(\varrho_{t})(\gamma_{i}(t))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq j\leq m\atop j\neq i}\frac{1}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t)}+\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right),\quad 1\leq i\leq m.

where the implicit constant in
\OO(\cdot)
depends only on
{\mathsf{T}}
. Since the density
\varrho_{t}
is bounded by
\sqrt{A/t}/2
(from (
2.45
), we have
|\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i\pm 1}(t)|\geq\sqrt{t/A}/n
, and
|\gamma_{i}(t)-(\pm 2\sqrt{At})|\geq\sqrt{t/A}/n
(which follows from (
2.14
) that
\int_{-2\sqrt{At}}^{\gamma_{i}(t)}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x,\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)}^{2\sqrt{At}}\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x\geq 1/(2n)
). We consider the integral

(2.47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{=}}\PV\int_{\gamma_{i-1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i+1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right)\\
&=\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)-\varrho_{t}(\gamma_{i}(t))}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx+\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{\sqrt{t/A}}{n}\max_{x:|x-\gamma_{i}(t)|\leq\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|+\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{tn}}+\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right)=\OO\left(\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\right).\end{split}

We used in the third line that on
[\gamma_{i}(t)+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n),\gamma_{i+1}(t)]
and
[\gamma_{i-1}(t),\gamma_{i}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)]

we have
|1/(\gamma_{i}(t)-x)|\leq 1/(\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)))=2n\sqrt{A/t}
. So

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\left|\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\right|+\left|\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2n\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i-1}}(t)}\varrho_{t}(x)dx+2n\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}\int_{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}^{{\gamma_{i}}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}\varrho_{t}(x)dx\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}},

where we also used the definition of classical locations from (
2.14
);
in the fourth line we used that
\PV\int_{\gamma_{i}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}^{{\gamma_{i}(t)}+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)}(\gamma_{i}(t)-x)^{-1}dx=0
; in the last line, we used that for
x\in[\gamma_{m}(t)-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n),\gamma_{1}(t)+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)]\subset[-2\sqrt{At}+\sqrt{t/A}/(2n),2\sqrt{At}-\sqrt{t/A}/(2n)]
, it holds that

|\varrho_{t}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\sqrt{An}/t
from (
2.45
); in the last equality we used that
A=m/n\geq 1/n
.
For the integral outside the interval
[\gamma_{i-1}(t),\gamma_{i+1}(t)]
, we have the trivial bounds

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}\leq\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\int_{\gamma_{j+1}(t)}^{\gamma_{j}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t)}\leq\sum_{j=i+1}^{m}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))},

and

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}\geq\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\int_{\gamma_{j+1}(t)}^{\gamma_{j}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j+1}(t)}\geq\sum_{j=i+2}^{m}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}.

Together with the fact that
\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i+1}(t)\geq\sqrt{t/A}/n
, this gives

(2.48)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\gamma_{i+1}(t)}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x)dx}{\gamma_{i}(t)-x}-\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t))}\right|\leq\frac{1}{n(\gamma_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i+1}(t))}\leq\sqrt{\frac{A}{t}}.

We have the same estimate for the integral from
\gamma_{i-1}(t)
to
\infty
. The claim (
2.46
) follows from combining (
2.47
) and (
2.48
).

Using (
2.46
) as input, following the derivations of (
2.41
) and (
2.42
), we conclude there exists a large constant
C>0
such that for any
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
and
n
large enough, the following holds with probability
1-Ce^{-(\log n)^{2}}
,

(2.49)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t,\atop 1\leq i\leq n}|\lambda_{i}(s)-\gamma_{i}(s)|\leq C\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{A{\rm d}\tau}{\sqrt{\tau}}+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)=C\left(2\sqrt{tA}+\frac{(\log n)\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

This finishes the proof of the first statement in (
2.43
). The second statement follows from the first one and the bound
|\gamma_{i}(s)|\leq 2\sqrt{As}\leq 2\sqrt{At}
.
∎

2.5.
Free Convolution Density Estimates

Adopt
1.2
. Denote the free convolution of
\mu_{0}:=\mu_{0}^{(n)}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. Denote the Stieltjes transform of
\mu_{t}
by
m_{t}(z)
as in (
2.1
), and its
p
-th derivative by
m_{t}^{(p)}(z)
. The free convolution
\mu_{t}=\varrho_{t}(x){\rm d}x
has a density
\varrho_{t}(x)
. Thanks to
Item

5
, the right edge of
\mu_{t}
, denoted by
E_{t}=\max(\supp\mu_{t})
, satisfies
E_{t}=\xi(t)-tm_{0}(\xi(t))
, where
\xi(t)
is given by

(2.50)

\displaystyle\xi(t)=\sup\Bigg\{w\in\mathbb{R}:\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{0}({\rm d}x)}{(x-w)^{2}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\Bigg\}.

The following two lemmas collects some properties of
\mu_{t}
, i.e., square root edge behavior and averaged density estimates. They will be used in
Section

3
to prove our main result
1.3
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Adopt
1.2
. Denote the free convolution of
\mu_{0}:=\mu_{0}^{(n)}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
, and its right edge by
E_{t}=\max(\supp\mu_{t})
. We denote the Stieltjes transform of
\mu_{t}
as
m_{t}(z)
, recall
\xi(t)
from (
2.50
), and introduce the following two parameters:

(2.51)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(t)=\frac{2}{-t^{3}m^{(2)}_{0}(\xi(t))},\quad{\mathfrak{c}}(t)=-2^{-9}tm^{(2)}_{0}(\xi(t))\xi(t)^{2}.

Then for any
(30/{\mathsf{b}})\sqrt{\eta}_{*}\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
we have

(2.52)

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\geq\xi(t)\geq\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}t}{20}\right)^{2},\quad{\mathcal{A}}(t)\geq 2^{-10}{\mathsf{b}}^{2},\quad{\mathfrak{c}}(t)\geq 2^{-20}({\mathsf{b}}t)^{2},

and for all
x\in[E_{t}-{\mathfrak{c}}(t),E_{t}]
we have

(2.53)

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}(x)=(1+{\mathcal{E}}(E_{t}-x))\frac{\sqrt{{\mathcal{A}}(t)(E_{t}-x)}}{\pi},\qquad\text{where $\mathcal{E}$ satisfies $|{\mathcal{E}}(x)|\leq\frac{|x|}{{\mathfrak{c}}(t)}$}.

Lemma 2.15
.

Adopt
1.2
. Denote the free convolution of
\mu_{0}:=\mu_{0}^{(n)}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
by
\mu_{t}=\mu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
, and its right edge by
E_{t}=\max(\supp\mu_{t})
. We denote the Stieltjes transform of
\mu_{t}
by
m_{t}
. Then there exists constants
{\mathfrak{C}}=2^{21}/{\mathsf{b}}^{2}>0
and
{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathsf{b}}{\mathfrak{C}}^{-3/2}>0
such that the following hold.

(1)

The right edge satisfies
E_{t}\leq 2\sqrt{t}
.

(2)

For any
t\in[0,{\mathsf{T}}]
and
x\in[0,{\mathsf{T}}^{2}]
satisfying either
t\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\sqrt{\eta}_{*}
or
x\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\eta_{*}
, we have
\mu_{t}([E_{t}-x,E_{t}])\geq{\mathfrak{c}}x^{3/2}
.

(3)

For any
t\in[0,{\mathsf{T}}]
and
\kappa,\eta\geq 0
with
\kappa+\eta\leq{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
satisfying either
t\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\sqrt{\eta}_{*}
or
\kappa\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\eta_{*}
, we have

(2.54)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}[m_{t}(E_{t}+\kappa+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\geq\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}\eta}{8\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}.

Proof of
2.14
.

For simplicity of notation, in the rest of the proof, we will write
\kappa=\xi(t)
as defined in (
2.50
). Let
F(x)=\mu_{0}([-x,0])
, which satisfies
F(x)\geq{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}
for
x\in[\eta_{*},\mathsf{T}^{2}]
. Then we have

(2.55)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\mu_{0}({\rm d}x)}{(\kappa-x)^{2}}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}F(x)}{(\kappa+x)^{2}}\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{2F(x){\rm d}x}{(\kappa+x)^{3}}\geq\int_{\eta_{*}}^{1}\frac{2{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}{\rm d}x}{(\kappa+x)^{3}},

where the first statement is from (
2.50
); the second statement is from the definition
F(x)=\mu_{0}([-x,0])
; the third statement is by integration by parts; and the last statement is by restricting the integration domain to
[\eta_{*},1]
, and using that
F(x)\geq{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}
on
[\eta_{*},{\mathsf{T}}]
and
{\mathsf{T}}\geq 1
from
1.2
.

If
\kappa\leq 2\eta_{*}
, then (
2.55
) gives

(2.56)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\geq\int_{\eta_{*}}^{1}\frac{2{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}{\rm d}x}{27x^{3}}=\frac{4{\mathsf{b}}}{27}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta}_{*}}-1\right)\geq\frac{{\mathsf{b}}}{27\sqrt{\eta}_{*}},

where the second statement is from performing the integral, and the last statement uses
\eta_{*}\leq 1/4
from
1.2
. This contradicts our assumption
t\geq(30/{\mathsf{b}})\sqrt{\eta_{*}}>27\sqrt{\eta}_{*}/{\mathsf{b}}
, so we have
\kappa\geq 2\eta_{*}
, and (
2.55
) gives

(2.57)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\geq\int_{\eta_{*}}^{\kappa}\frac{2{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}{\rm d}x}{8\kappa^{3}}=\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\kappa^{5/2}-\eta_{*}^{5/2})}{10\kappa^{3}}\geq\frac{{\mathsf{b}}}{20\kappa^{1/2}},\qquad\text{so}\qquad\kappa\geq\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}t}{20}\right)^{2},

where the first inequality follows from restricting the integral (
2.55
) to
[\eta_{*},\kappa]
and the fact that
\kappa+x\leq 2\kappa
on this region; the second statement follows from performing the integral; and the last inequality follows from the bound
\kappa\geq 2\eta_{*}
. This gives the lower bound in the first statement in (
2.52
). The upper bound follows from the following estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\mu_{0}({\rm d}x)}{(\kappa-x)^{2}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\mu_{0}({\rm d}x)}{\kappa^{2}}=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}

where the first statement is from (
2.50
); the second statement uses
\kappa-x\geq\kappa
; and the last statement uses
\mu_{0}({\mathbb{R}})=1
.

We next bound the derivatives of
m_{0}
, in order to Taylor expand it. In the following, we recall that the
p
-th derivative of
m_{0}
is denoted by
m_{0}^{(p)}
. Since
m_{0}(w)
is analytic outside
\supp\mu_{0}\subset(-\infty,0]
, it is in particular analytic on
\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:|w-\kappa|<\kappa/2\}
. To bound
m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)
observe that, similarly to (
2.55
) and (
2.57
), we have

(2.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}-m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)&=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{6F(x)\mathrm{d}x}{(\kappa+x)^{4}}\geq\int_{\eta_{*}}^{\kappa}\frac{6{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}{\rm d}x}{(\kappa+x)^{4}}\geq\int^{\kappa}_{\eta_{*}}\frac{4{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}{\rm d}x}{(2\kappa)^{4}}=\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\kappa^{5/2}-\eta_{*}^{5/2})}{10\kappa^{4}}\geq\frac{{\mathsf{b}}}{20\kappa^{3/2}},\end{split}

where in the second statement we restricted the integral to
[\eta_{*},\kappa]
, and used
\kappa\leq\sqrt{t}\leq\sqrt{\mathsf{T}}\leq{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
from the first statement in (
2.52
), and
F(x)=\mu_{0}([-x,0])\geq{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}
from
1.2
; in the third statement we used
\kappa+x\leq 2\kappa
; and in the last two statements used that
\kappa\geq 2\eta_{*}
. We also have the upper bounds of
-m_{0}^{(2)}

(2.59)

\displaystyle-m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}2!\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{(\kappa-x)^{3}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}2!\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{(\kappa-x)^{2}\kappa}=\frac{2}{t\kappa},

and

(2.60)

\displaystyle m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}3!\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{(\kappa-x)^{4}}\leq 6\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{\kappa(\kappa-x)^{3}}=\frac{-3m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}{\kappa}.

where we bounded
|\kappa-x|\geq\kappa
for
x\in\supp\mu_{0}\subset(-\infty,0]
(to obtain both estimates) and used (
2.50
) (in the last statement of (
2.59
)).
For any
w\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
|w-\kappa|<\kappa/2
, we have

(2.61)

\displaystyle|m_{0}^{(4)}(w)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}4!\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{|x-w|^{5}}\leq 4!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2^{3}{\rm d}\mu_{0}(x)}{(\kappa/2)^{2}(x-\kappa)^{3}}=\frac{-3\times 2^{7}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}{\kappa^{2}},

where we used that
|x-w|\geq\kappa/2
and
2|x-w|\geq|x-\kappa|
for
x\in\supp\mu_{0}\subseteq(-\infty,0]
for the second inequality.

We can thus Taylor expand the Stieltjes transform
m_{0}(w)
of
\mu_{0}
around
\kappa
when
|w-\kappa|<\kappa/2
:

(2.62)

\displaystyle\begin{split}m_{0}(w)&=m_{0}(\kappa)+m_{0}^{\prime}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)+m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{2}}{2}+m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{3}}{3!}+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(w)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{4}}{4!}\\
&=m_{0}(\kappa)+\frac{(w-\kappa)}{t}+m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{2}}{2}+m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{3}}{3!}+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(w)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{4}}{4!},\end{split}

where in the second line we used that
\kappa=\xi(t)
and
m_{0}^{\prime}(\xi(t))=1/t
from the definition of
\xi(t)
(
2.50
). Moreover, thanks to (
2.61
), the Taylor remainder is bounded by

(2.63)

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(z)|\leq\max_{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:|w-\kappa|\leq\kappa/2}|m_{0}^{(4)}(w)|\leq\frac{-3\times 2^{7}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}{\kappa^{2}}.

By
Item

4
in
2.3
, any
x\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\varrho_{t}(E_{t}-x)>0
if and only if there exists a
w\in\del\Lambda_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}
such that
E_{t}-x=w-tm_{0}(w)
. Recalling that
E_{t}=\kappa-tm_{0}(\kappa)
(from
\kappa=\xi(t)
and
Item

5
in
2.3
), rearranging (
2.62
) gives

(2.64)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathbb{R}}\ni x&=(\kappa-tm_{0}(\kappa))-(w-tm_{0}(w))\\
&=tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{2}}{2}+tm_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{3}}{3!}+t\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(w)\frac{(w-\kappa)^{4}}{4!}\\
&=\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)\frac{tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}}{2},\end{split}

where

(2.65)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(w):=\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\frac{(w-\kappa)}{3}+\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(w)}{m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\frac{(w-\kappa)^{2}}{12}.

When
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
, from (
2.65
) we have

(2.66)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|\leq\frac{|w-\kappa|}{\kappa}+\frac{2^{5}|w-\kappa|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\leq\frac{5|w-\kappa|}{\kappa}\leq\frac{5}{8}<1,

where we used (
2.60
) and (
2.63
) for the first inequality, and
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
for the second. Next we show that the map
w\mapsto\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2
is a surjection from
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
onto
\{u\in{\mathbb{C}}:|u|\leq-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}\}
.

Fix any
|u|\leq-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}
; we will use Rouché’s theorem to compare the two holomorphic functions
tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u
and
\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u
on the domain
\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa\}
. First we notice that
tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u
has a root with
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
. Next, for any
w
such that
|w-\kappa|=2^{-3}\kappa
, we can upper bound the difference

(2.67)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\left(\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u\right)-\left(tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u\right)\right|\\
&=-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)|w-\kappa|^{2}|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|/2\leq-(5/8)\cdot tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)|w-\kappa|^{2}/2\leq\left|tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u\right|,\end{split}

where in the second inequality we used (
2.66
) indicating that
|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|\leq 5/8
, and in the last inequality we used that, when
|w-\kappa|=2^{-3}\kappa
,

(2.68)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{8}\frac{tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)|w-\kappa|^{2}}{2}=-\frac{3}{8}\frac{tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)2^{-6}\kappa^{2}}{2}>-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}\geq|u|.

The relation (
2.67
) verifies the conditions of Rouché’s theorem, and hence
\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)(w-\kappa)^{2}/2-u
also has a root with
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
.

In particular for any
0\leq x\leq-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}
, (
2.64
) has a solution
w=w(x)
with
|w-\kappa|\leq 2^{-3}\kappa
:

(2.69)

\displaystyle w-\kappa=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)^{-1/2},\quad|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|\leq 5/8.

To further simplify (
2.69
), we will use the following elementary inequalities: for any
|\varepsilon|\leq 5/8
.

(2.70)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon}}-1\right|\leq 2\varepsilon,\quad\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon}}-1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right|\leq\frac{8\varepsilon^{2}}{3},

Using them we can rewrite
{\mathcal{E}}(w)
from (
2.65
) as

(2.71)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)^{-1/2}-1+\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{6m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}\right|\\
&\leq\left|\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)^{-1/2}-1+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(w)}{2}\right|+\left|\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(w)}{2}-\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\frac{(w-\kappa)}{6}\right|+\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{6|m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)|}\left|w-\kappa-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}\right|\\
&\leq\frac{8|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|^{2}}{3}+\frac{|\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(w)|}{m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\frac{|w-\kappa|^{2}}{24}+\frac{3}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}\frac{|{\mathcal{E}}(w)|}{3}\\
&\leq\frac{|w-\kappa|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\frac{200}{3}+\frac{16|w-\kappa|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}+\frac{5|w-\kappa|}{\kappa^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}\\
&\leq\frac{2x}{-t\kappa^{2}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\left(\frac{248}{3|1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)|}+\frac{5}{|1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)|^{1/2}}\right)\leq\frac{2^{9}x}{-t\kappa^{2}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)},\end{split}

where in the second line we used the triangle inquality; in the third line we used (
2.60
), the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}(w)
from (
2.65
), (
2.69
) and (
2.70
); in the fourth line we used (
2.66
) for the first and third terms and (
2.63
) for the second term; in the last line we used (
2.69
) and
1/|1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)|\leq 8/3
from (
2.66
).

By plugging (
2.71
) into (
2.69
) and using (
4a
) in
2.3
, we conclude that for any
0\leq x\leq-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\varrho_{t}(E_{t}-x)=\frac{{\rm Im}[w]}{t\pi}=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-t^{3}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}{\rm Re}\left[\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w)\right)^{-1/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-t^{3}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}{\rm Re}\left[1-\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{6m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}+\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)\right]=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-t^{3}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}}(1+{\rm Re}[\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)])

where for the last equality, we used that the middle term inside
{\rm Re}[\cdot]
is purely imaginary, and introduced the notation

(2.72)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)=\left(1+{\mathcal{E}}(w(x))\right)^{-1/2}-1+\frac{m_{0}^{(3)}(\kappa)}{6m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{-tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)}},\quad|\Real\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)|\leq|\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)|\leq\frac{2^{9}|x|}{-t\kappa^{2}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)},

where the bound of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(x)
is from (
2.71
).
This gives the statement (
2.53
).
Using (
2.59
) and the lower bound
\kappa\geq({\mathsf{b}}t/20)^{2}
from (
2.57
), get

{\mathcal{A}}(t)=-2/(t^{3}m_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa))\geq\kappa/t^{2}\geq{\mathsf{b}}^{2}/400\geq 2^{-10}{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
. Using the lower bound (
2.58
) and (
2.57
), we have
{\mathfrak{c}}(t)=-2^{-9}tm_{0}^{(2)}(\kappa)\kappa^{2}\geq 2^{-9}t{\mathsf{b}}\kappa^{1/2}/20\geq 2^{-9}t^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{2}/400\geq 2^{-20}({\mathsf{b}}t)^{2}
. These two estimates give the last two statements in (
2.52
).
∎

Proof of
2.15
.

We denote the inverted cumulative density (recall from (
2.13
)) associated with
\{\mu_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}}
, by
\gamma_{t}:[0,1]\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
for
0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}
.
Let
F(x)=\mu_{0}([-x,0])\geq{\mathsf{b}}x^{3/2}
for
\eta_{*}\leq x\leq{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
.

The first statement follows from a comparison argument using
2.11
. Denote
\breve{\mu}_{0}:=\delta_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
, and let
\breve{\mu}_{t}=\breve{\mu}_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
be the free convolution of
\breve{\mu}_{0}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. Then recall from
Example

2.5
(with
A=1
) that
\breve{\mu}_{t}
is given explicitly by

\displaystyle{\rm d}\breve{\mu}_{t}=\frac{\sqrt{4t-x^{2}}}{2\pi t}{\rm d}x.

For the measure valued process
\{\breve{\mu}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}}
, we denote the associated inverted cumulative density function by
\breve{\gamma}_{t}:[0,1]\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
. From construction, for
y\in[0,1]
we have
\breve{\gamma}_{0}(y)=0\geq\gamma_{0}(y)
. Thus Item
1
in
2.11
implies that for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle E_{t}=\gamma_{t}(0)\leq\breve{\gamma}_{t}(0)=2\sqrt{t}.

This gives the first statement in
2.15
.

For the second claim, we analyze the two scenarios; the first is if
t\leq\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}x}
, and the second is if
t\geq\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}x}
.
If
t\leq\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}x}
, then we either have
{\mathfrak{C}}\sqrt{\eta}_{*}\leq t\leq\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}x}
or
x\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\eta_{*}
. In both cases
x\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\eta_{*}
. We will prove the claim
\mu_{t}(E_{t}-x,E_{t})\geq{\mathfrak{c}}x^{3/2}
by using the comparison results from
2.11
.

From the discussion above (and our assumption in
2.15
), in the case that
t\leq\sqrt{{\mathfrak{C}}x}
, we have
{\mathsf{T}}^{2}\geq x\geq{\mathfrak{C}}\eta_{*}
. By
1.2
we have
1\geq\mu_{0}([-x/{\mathfrak{C}},0])\geq{\mathsf{b}}(x/{\mathfrak{C}})^{3/2}
for any
x/{\mathfrak{C}}\leq{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
. We denote

A:={\mathsf{b}}(x/{\mathfrak{C}})^{3/2}\leq 1
. Then
\gamma_{0}(A)\geq-x/{\mathfrak{C}}
. Let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
be the restriction of
\gamma_{0}
to
[0,A]
; then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
is the inverted height function of a finite measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{\rm fin}
(by the definition (
2.13
),
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
is the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,A]
by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
), and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})=A
. We also denote by
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}=\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
the free convolution of
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
with the rescaled semicircle distribution
\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
. Then
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a measure-valued process with constant total mass
A
. We denote the associated inverted cumulative density function (recall from (
2.13
)) of
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq{\mathsf{T}}}
by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}:[0,A]\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
. Then from construction we have
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(y)=\gamma_{0}(y)
for
y\in[0,A]
. Thus for
y,y^{\prime}\in[0,A]
we have
